***The New York Times*** (***NYT***)[^cite_note-6] is a newspaper based in [Manhattan](45470.md), New York City. *The New York Times* covers domestic, national, and international news, and publishes opinion pieces and reviews. One of the longest-running newspapers in the United States, the *Times* serves as one of the country's [newspapers of record](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newspaper_of_record). As of June 2026, *The New York Times* had 13.35 million total subscribers: 550,000 print and 12.8 million digital, both the [highest numbers for any newspaper in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_newspapers_in_the_United_States) by a significant margin. *The New York Times* is published by [the New York Times Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Company). Since 1896, the company has been chaired by the Ochs-Sulzberger family. The current chairman and the paper's publisher is [A. G. Sulzberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._G._Sulzberger). The *Times* is headquartered at [The New York Times Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Building) in [Midtown Manhattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midtown_Manhattan).

The *Times* was founded as the conservative *New-York Daily Times* in 1851, and came to national recognition in the 1870s with its aggressive coverage of corrupt politician [Boss Tweed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_M._Tweed). Following the [Panic of 1893](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panic_of_1893), *[Chattanooga Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chattanooga_Times)* publisher [Adolph Ochs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolph_Ochs) gained a controlling interest in the company. In 1935, Ochs was succeeded by his son-in-law, [Arthur Hays Sulzberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Hays_Sulzberger), who began a push into European news. Sulzberger's son [Arthur Ochs Sulzberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Ochs_Sulzberger) became publisher in 1963, adapting to a changing newspaper industry and introducing radical changes. *The New York Times* was involved in the landmark 1964 [U.S. Supreme Court](31737.md) case *[New York Times Co. v. Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Times_Co._v._Sullivan)*, which restricted the ability of public officials to sue the media for [defamation](28661.md).

In 1971, *The New York Times* published the *[Pentagon Papers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentagon_Papers)*, an internal [Department of Defense](7279897.md) document detailing the [United States's historical involvement](8453309.md) in the [Vietnam War](32611.md), despite pushback from then-president [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon). In the landmark decision *[New York Times Co. v. United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Times_Co._v._United_States)* (1971), the Supreme Court ruled that the [First Amendment](31653.md) guaranteed the right to publish the *Pentagon Papers*. In the 1980s, the *Times* began a two-decade progression to digital technology and launched nytimes.com in 1996. In the 21st century, it shifted its publication online amid the global [decline of newspapers](22025015.md).

Currently, the *Times* maintains several regional bureaus staffed with journalists across six continents. It has expanded to several other publications, including *[The New York Times Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Magazine)*, *[The New York Times International Edition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_International_Edition)*, and *[The New York Times Book Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Book_Review)*. In addition, the paper has produced several television series, podcasts—including *[The Daily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Daily_\(podcast\))*—and games through *[The New York Times Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Games)*. Among other accolades, it has been awarded the Pulitzer Prize [135 times since 1918](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Pulitzer_Prizes_awarded_to_The_New_York_Times), the most of any publication. However, *The New York Times* has been involved in [a number of controversies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_controversies_involving_The_New_York_Times) in its history. According to a 2025 [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) study on educational differences among audiences of 30 major U.S. [news outlets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/News_media), *The New York Times* had the highest proportion of [college-educated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Undergraduate_education) readers among the daily newspapers surveyed, with 56% of its audience holding at least a bachelor's degree.[^cite_note-7]

*The New York Times* is sometimes referred to as "The Gray Lady", a nickname which references both the color of newsprint and the *Times*' opposition to [yellow journalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_journalism).[^cite_note-8][^cite_note-9]

## History

### 1851–1896

Main article: [History of *The New York Times* (1851–1896)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_The_New_York_Times_\(1851%E2%80%931896\))



This pre-publication prospectus presented the newspaper's philosophies, including that it "will canvass freely the character and pretensions of Public Men, the merits and demerits of all administrations of Government, National, State and Municipal, and the worth of all institutions, principles, habits and professions".

[^cite_note-NYTimes_20260104-10]



The first issue of *The New York Times*—initially called the *New-York Daily Times*—was published on Thursday, September 18, 1851. The name was changed in 1857 to *The New-York Times* after the evening edition was suspended, and the hyphen was dropped in 1896.

[^cite_note-NYTimes_20260104-10]

*The New York Times* was established in 1851 as the *New-York Daily Times* by *[New-York Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New-York_Tribune)* journalists [Henry Jarvis Raymond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Jarvis_Raymond) and [George Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Jones_\(publisher\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger19514–5-11]

Financier [Edward B. Wesley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_B._Wesley) is credited as the third co-founder, in his capacity as the original business manager and the provider of half the starting capital. Wesley sold his interest in the newspaper in 1860.[^cite_note-12][^cite_note-13][^cite_note-14][^cite_note-15][^cite_note-16]

The *Times* experienced significant circulation, particularly among conservatives; *New-York Tribune* publisher [Horace Greeley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horace_Greeley) praised the *Times*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger19517–9-17] During the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War), *Times* correspondents gathered information directly from [Confederate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_States_of_America) states.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavis192156–57-18] In 1869, Jones inherited the paper from Raymond,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger195132-19] who had changed its name to *The New-York Times*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger195121-20] Under Jones, the *Times* began to publish a series of articles criticizing [Tammany Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tammany_Hall) political boss [William M. Tweed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_M._Tweed), despite vehement opposition from other New York newspapers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger195135-21] In 1871, *The New-York Times* published Tammany Hall's accounting books; Tweed was tried in 1873 and sentenced to twelve years in prison. The *Times* earned national recognition for its coverage of Tweed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger195144–51-22] In 1891, Jones died, creating a management imbroglio in which his children had insufficient business acumen to inherit the company and his will prevented an acquisition of the *Times*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavis1921167–168-23] Editor-in-chief [Charles Ransom Miller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Ransom_Miller), editorial editor Edward Cary, and correspondent George F. Spinney established a company to manage *The New-York Times*,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavis1921170-24] but faced financial difficulties during the [Panic of 1893](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panic_of_1893).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavis1921171-25]

### 1896–1945

Main article: [History of *The New York Times* (1896–1945)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_The_New_York_Times_\(1896%E2%80%931945\))

In August 1896, *[Chattanooga Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chattanooga_Times)* publisher [Adolph Ochs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolph_Ochs) acquired *The New-York Times*, implementing significant alterations to the newspaper's structure. Ochs established the *Times* as a merchant's newspaper and removed the hyphen from the newspaper's name.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger1951105–110-26] In 1905, *The New York Times* opened [Times Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Times_Square), marking expansion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger1951153-27] The *Times* experienced a political realignment in the 1910s amid several disagreements within the [Republican Party](32070.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavis1921250–252-28] *The New York Times* reported on the [sinking of the *Titanic*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinking_of_the_Titanic), as other newspapers were cautious about bulletins circulated by the [Associated Press](18935732.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger1951193–197-29] Through managing editor [Carr Van Anda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carr_Van_Anda), the *Times* paid considerable attention to advances in science, reporting on [Albert Einstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Einstein)'s then-obscure theory of [general relativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_relativity) and becoming involved in the [discovery of the tomb of Tutankhamun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discovery_of_the_tomb_of_Tutankhamun).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger1951250–252-30] In April 1935, Ochs died, leaving his son-in-law [Arthur Hays Sulzberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Hays_Sulzberger) as publisher.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger1951403–409-31] The [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) forced Sulzberger to reduce *The New York Times*'s operations,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger1951422–423-32] and developments in the New York newspaper landscape resulted in the formation of larger newspapers, such as the *[New York Herald Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Herald_Tribune)* and the *[New York World-Telegram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_World-Telegram)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger1951369–372-33] In contrast to Ochs, Sulzberger encouraged [wirephotography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wirephoto).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger1951412-34]

*The New York Times* extensively covered [World War II](32927.md) through large headlines,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger1951433–436-35] reporting on exclusive stories such as the [Yugoslav coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugoslav_coup_d'%C3%A9tat).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger1951446-36] Amid the war, Sulzberger began expanding the *Times*'s operations further, acquiring [WQXR-FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WQXR-FM) in 1944—the first non-*Times* investment since the Jones era—and established a fashion show in Times Hall. Despite reductions as a result of conscription, *The New York Times* retained the largest journalism staff of any newspaper.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger1951493–495-37] The *Times*'s print edition became available internationally during the war through the [Army & Air Force Exchange Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Army_&_Air_Force_Exchange_Service); *The New York Times Overseas Weekly* later became available in Japan through *[The Asahi Shimbun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Asahi_Shimbun)* and in Germany through the *[Frankfurter Zeitung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frankfurter_Zeitung)*. The international edition would develop into [a separate newspaper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_International_Edition).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunlap2015b-38] Journalist [William L. Laurence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_L._Laurence) publicized the [atomic bomb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_bomb) race between the United States and Germany, resulting in the [Federal Bureau of Investigation](11127.md) seizing copies of the *Times*. The United States government recruited Laurence to document the [Manhattan Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manhattan_Project) in April 1945.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger1951510–515-39] Laurence became the only witness of the Manhattan Project, a detail realized by employees of *The New York Times* following the [atomic bombing of Hiroshima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_bombing_of_Hiroshima).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger1951522–523-40]

### 1945–1998

Main article: [History of *The New York Times* (1945–1998)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_The_New_York_Times_\(1945%E2%80%931998\))

Following [World War II](32927.md), *The New York Times* continued to expand.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger1951541–542-41] The *Times* was subject to investigations from the [Senate Internal Security Subcommittee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate_Subcommittee_on_Internal_Security), a [McCarthyist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McCarthyism) subcommittee that investigated purported communism from within press institutions. [Arthur Hays Sulzberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Hays_Sulzberger)'s decision to dismiss a copyreader who had pleaded the [Fifth Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution) drew ire from within the *Times* and from external organizations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETalese1981289-42] In April 1961, Sulzberger resigned, appointing his son-in-law, [The New York Times Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Company) president [Orvil Dryfoos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orvil_Dryfoos).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETalese198127-43] Under Dryfoos, *The New York Times* established a newspaper based in Los Angeles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETalese1981343-44] In 1962, the implementation of automated [printing presses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Printing_press) in response to increasing costs mounted fears over [technological unemployment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technological_unemployment). The New York Typographical Union staged [a strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1962%E2%80%931963_New_York_City_newspaper_strike) in December, altering the media consumption of New Yorkers. The strike left New York with three remaining newspapers—the *Times*, the [*Daily News*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Daily_News), and the *[New York Post](102227.md)*—by its conclusion in March 1963.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETalese1981364–368-45] In May, Dryfoos died of a heart ailment.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETalese1981396-46] Following weeks of ambiguity, [Arthur Ochs Sulzberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Ochs_Sulzberger) became *The New York Times*'s publisher.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETalese1981380–383-47]

Technological advancements leveraged by newspapers such as the *[Los Angeles Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Times)* and improvements in coverage from *[The Washington Post](102226.md)* and *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)* necessitated adaptations to nascent computing.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETalese1981403–405-48] *The New York Times* published "[Heed Their Rising Voices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heed_Their_Rising_Voices)" in 1960, a full-page advertisement purchased by supporters of [Martin Luther King Jr.](20076.md) criticizing law enforcement in [Montgomery, Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montgomery,_Alabama) for their response to the [civil rights movement](49001.md). Montgomery Public Safety commissioner L. B. Sullivan sued the *Times* for defamation. In *[New York Times Co. v. Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Times_Co._v._Sullivan)* (1964), the [U.S. Supreme Court](31737.md) ruled that the verdict in Alabama county court and the [Supreme Court of Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Alabama) violated the [First Amendment](31653.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunlap2017c-49] The decision is considered to be a [landmark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_landmark_court_decisions_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELiptak2021-50] After financial losses, *The New York Times* ended its [international edition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_International_Edition), acquiring a stake in the *[Paris Herald Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Herald_Tribune)*, forming the *[International Herald Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Herald_Tribune)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETalese1981545-51] The *Times* initially published the *[Pentagon Papers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentagon_Papers)*, facing opposition from then-president [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon). The Supreme Court ruled in *The New York Times*'s favor in *[New York Times Co. v. United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Times_Co._v._United_States)* (1971), allowing the *Times* and *The Washington Post* to publish the papers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChokshi2017-52]

*The New York Times* remained cautious in its initial coverage of the [Watergate scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watergate_scandal).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhelps2009166–169-53] As [Congress](31756.md) began investigating the scandal, the *Times* furthered its coverage,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhelps2009186–187-54] publishing details on the [Huston Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huston_Plan), alleged wiretapping of reporters and officials,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhelps2009191-55] and testimony from [James W. McCord Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_W._McCord_Jr.) that the [Committee for the Re-Election of the President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_for_the_Re-Election_of_the_President) paid the conspirators off.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhelps2009189-56] The exodus of readers to suburban New York newspapers, such as *[Newsday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newsday)* and [Gannett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gannett) papers, adversely affected *The New York Times*'s circulation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney202322–24-57] Contemporary newspapers balked at additional sections; *[Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\))* devoted a cover for its criticism and *[New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_\(magazine\))* wrote that the *Times* was engaging in "middle-class self-absorption".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunlap2015a-58] *The New York Times*, the *Daily News*, and the *New York Post* were the subject of [a strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978_New_York_City_newspaper_strike) in 1978,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDewar1978-59] allowing emerging newspapers to leverage halted coverage.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStetson1978-60] The *Times* deliberately avoided coverage of the [AIDS epidemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIDS_epidemic), running its first front-page article in May 1983. [Max Frankel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Frankel)'s editorial coverage of the epidemic, with mentions of [anal intercourse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anal_intercourse), contrasted with then-executive editor [A. M. Rosenthal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._M._Rosenthal)'s puritan approach, intentionally avoiding descriptions of the luridity of gay venues.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney202354–56-61]

Following years of waning interest in *The New York Times*, Sulzberger resigned in January 1992, appointing his son, [Arthur Ochs Sulzberger Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Ochs_Sulzberger_Jr.), as publisher.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney2023131-62] The Internet represented a generational shift within the *Times*; Sulzberger, who negotiated The New York Times Company's acquisition of *[The Boston Globe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Boston_Globe)* in 1993, derided the Internet, while his son expressed antithetical views. @times appeared on [America Online](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/America_Online)'s website in May 1994 as an extension of *The New York Times*, featuring news articles, film reviews, sports news, and business articles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney2023146–148-63] Despite opposition, several employees of the *Times* had begun to access the Internet.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunlap2017a-64] The online success of publications that traditionally co-existed with the *Times*—such as America Online, [Yahoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahoo), and [CNN](62028.md)—and the expansion of websites such as [Monster.com](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monster.com) and [Craigslist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Craigslist) that threatened *The New York Times*'s [classified advertisement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classified_advertising) model increased efforts to develop a website.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney2023173–175-65] [nytimes.com](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nytimes.com) debuted on January 19 and was formally announced three days later.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney2023181–182-66] The *Times* published domestic terrorist [Ted Kaczynski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Kaczynski)'s essay *[Industrial Society and Its Future](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Society_and_Its_Future)* in 1995, contributing to his arrest after his brother [David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Kaczynski) recognized the essay's penmanship.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFarhi2015-67]

### 1998–present

Main article: [History of *The New York Times* (1998–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_The_New_York_Times_\(1998%E2%80%93present\))

Following the establishment of [nytimes.com](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nytimes.com), *The New York Times* retained its journalistic hesitancy under executive editor [Joseph Lelyveld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Lelyveld), refusing to publish an article reporting on the [Clinton–Lewinsky scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton%E2%80%93Lewinsky_scandal) from [Drudge Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drudge_Report). nytimes.com editors conflicted with print editors on several occasions, including wrongfully naming security guard [Richard Jewell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Jewell) as the suspect in the [Centennial Olympic Park bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centennial_Olympic_Park_bombing) and covering the [death of Diana, Princess of Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_of_Diana,_Princess_of_Wales) in greater detail than the print edition.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney2023186–190-68] The New York Times Electronic Media Company was adversely affected by the [dot-com crash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dot-com_crash).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney2023221–222-69] The *Times* extensively covered the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md). The following day's print issue contained sixty-six articles,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney2023249-70] the work of over three hundred dispatched reporters.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMnookin200461-71] Journalist [Judith Miller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judith_Miller) was the recipient of a package containing a white powder during the [2001 anthrax attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_anthrax_attacks), furthering anxiety within *The New York Times*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney2023255–256-72] In September 2002, Miller and military correspondent [Michael R. Gordon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_R._Gordon) wrote an article for the *Times* claiming that Iraq had purchased [aluminum tubes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_aluminum_tubes). The article was cited by then-president [George W. Bush](3414021.md) to claim that Iraq was constructing [weapons of mass destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_and_weapons_of_mass_destruction); the theoretical use of aluminum tubes to produce nuclear material was speculation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney2023276–278-73] In March 2003, the United States [invaded Iraq](201936.md), beginning the [Iraq War](5043324.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney2023281-74]

*The New York Times* attracted controversy after thirty-six articles[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESullivan2013a-75] from journalist [Jayson Blair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jayson_Blair) were discovered to be plagiarized.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMnookin2004171-76] Criticism over then-executive editor [Howell Raines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howell_Raines) and then-managing editor [Gerald M. Boyd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_M._Boyd) mounted following the scandal, culminating in a town hall in which a deputy editor criticized Raines for failing to question Blair's sources in article he wrote on the [D.C. sniper attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D.C._sniper_attacks).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMnookin2004183-77] In June 2003, Raines and Boyd resigned.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMnookin2004210–212-78] [Arthur Ochs Sulzberger Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Ochs_Sulzberger_Jr.) appointed [Bill Keller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Keller) as executive editor.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMnookin2004217-79] Miller continued to report on the Iraq War as a [journalistic embed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embedded_journalism) covering the country's weapons of mass destruction program. Keller and then-Washington bureau chief [Jill Abramson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jill_Abramson) unsuccessfully attempted to quell criticism. Conservative media criticized the *Times* over its coverage of [missing explosives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Qa'qaa_high_explosives_controversy) from the [Al Qa'qaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Qa'qaa) weapons facility.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney2023340–343-80] An article in December 2005 disclosing [warrantless surveillance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NSA_warrantless_surveillance_\(2001%E2%80%932007\)) by the [National Security Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Security_Agency) contributed to further criticism from the George W. Bush administration and the [Senate](24909346.md)'s refusal to renew the [Patriot Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriot_Act).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney2023354–355-81] In the [Plame affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plame_affair), [a Central Intelligence Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plame_affair_grand_jury_investigation) inquiry found that Miller had become aware of [Valerie Plame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valerie_Plame)'s identity through then-vice president [Dick Cheney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick_Cheney)'s chief of staff [Scooter Libby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scooter_Libby), resulting in Miller's resignation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney2023361–363-82]

During the [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession), *The New York Times* suffered significant fiscal difficulties as a consequence of the [subprime mortgage crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subprime_mortgage_crisis) and a decline in [classified advertising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classified_advertising).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney2023374–376-83] Exacerbated by [Rupert Murdoch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupert_Murdoch)'s revitalization of *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)* through his acquisition of [Dow Jones & Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dow_Jones_&_Company), [The New York Times Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Company) began enacting measures to reduce the newsroom budget. The company was forced to borrow \$250 million (equivalent to \$373.84 million in 2025) from Mexican billionaire [Carlos Slim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Slim) and fired over one hundred employees by 2010.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney2023378–381-84] nytimes.com's coverage of the [Eliot Spitzer prostitution scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eliot_Spitzer_prostitution_scandal), resulting in the resignation of then-New York governor [Eliot Spitzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eliot_Spitzer), furthered the legitimacy of the website as a journalistic medium.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney2023382–383-85] The *Times*'s economic downturn renewed discussions of an online paywall;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETimmer2010-86] *The New York Times* implemented a paywall in March 2011.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney2023398-87] Abramson succeeded Keller,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney2023401-88] continuing her characteristic investigations into corporate and government malfeasance into the *Times*'s coverage.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney2023413-89] Following conflicts with newly appointed chief executive [Mark Thompson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Thompson_\(media_executive\))'s ambitions,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney2023414–416-90] Abramson was dismissed by Sulzberger Jr., who named [Dean Baquet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dean_Baquet) as her replacement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarrSomaiya2014-91]

Leading up to the [2016 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election), *The New York Times* elevated the [Hillary Clinton email controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton_email_controversy) into a national issue.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKirby2017-92] [Donald Trump](4848272.md)'s upset victory contributed to an increase in subscriptions to the *Times*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAssociated_Press2016-93] *The New York Times* experienced unprecedented indignation from Trump, who referred to publications such as the *Times* as "[enemies of the people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enemy_of_the_people)" at the [Conservative Political Action Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Political_Action_Conference) and tweeted his disdain for the newspaper and [CNN](62028.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavisGrynbaum2017-94] In October 2017, *The New York Times* published an article by journalists [Jodi Kantor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jodi_Kantor) and [Megan Twohey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megan_Twohey) alleging that dozens of women had accused film producer and [The Weinstein Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Weinstein_Company) co-chairman [Harvey Weinstein](1624086.md) of sexual misconduct.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKantorTwohey2017-95] The investigation resulted in Weinstein's resignation and conviction,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPaiGrady2020-96] precipitated the [Weinstein effect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weinstein_effect),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiaz2022b-97] and served as a catalyst for the [#MeToo movement](55551931.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECBS_News2017-98] The New York Times Company vacated the public editor position[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVictor2017-99] and eliminated the copy desk in November.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchmidt2017-100] Sulzberger Jr. announced his resignation in December 2017, appointing his son, [A. G. Sulzberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._G._Sulzberger), as publisher.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmber2017b-101]

Trump's relationship—equally diplomatic and negative—marked Sulzberger's tenure.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStelter2018-102] In September 2018, *The New York Times* published "[I Am Part of the Resistance Inside the Trump Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I_Am_Part_of_the_Resistance_Inside_the_Trump_Administration)", an [anonymous essay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anonymous_essay) by a self-described Trump administration official later revealed to be [Department of Homeland Security](58236.md) chief of staff [Miles Taylor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miles_Taylor_\(security_expert\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShear2020-103] The animosity—which extended to nearly three hundred instances of Trump disparaging the *Times* by May 2019[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELeeQuealy2016-104]—culminated in Trump ordering federal agencies to cancel their subscriptions to *The New York Times* and *[The Washington Post](102226.md)* in October 2019.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFarhi2019-105] [Trump's tax returns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_returns_of_Donald_Trump) have been the subject of three separate investigations.[^cite_note-109] During the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md), the *Times* began implementing data services and graphs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliamsFehr2021-110] On May 23, 2020, *The New York Times*'s front page solely featured *[U.S. Deaths Near 100,000, An Incalculable Loss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Deaths_Near_100,000,_An_Incalculable_Loss)*, a subset of the 100,000 people in the United States who died of COVID-19, the first time that the *Times*'s front page lacked images since they were introduced.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrippe2020-111] Since 2020, *The New York Times* has focused on broader diversification, developing online games and producing television series.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatel2023-112] The New York Times Company acquired *[The Athletic](54936152.md)* in January 2022.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKafka2022a-113]

## Organization

### Management

Main article: [The New York Times Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Company)



[The New York Times Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Building) in Midtown Manhattan, New York

Since 1896, *The New York Times* has been published by the Ochs-Sulzberger family, having previously been published by [Henry Jarvis Raymond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Jarvis_Raymond) until 1869[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger195131-114] and by [George Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Jones_\(publisher\)) until 1896.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger1951105-115] [Adolph Ochs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolph_Ochs) published the *Times* until his death in 1935,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times1935-116] when he was succeeded by his son-in-law, [Arthur Hays Sulzberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Hays_Sulzberger). Sulzberger was publisher until 1961[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times1968-117] and was succeeded by [Orvil Dryfoos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orvil_Dryfoos), his son-in-law, who served in the position until his death in 1963.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times1963-118] [Arthur Ochs Sulzberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Ochs_Sulzberger) succeeded Dryfoos until his resignation in 1992.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaberman2012-119] His son, [Arthur Ochs Sulzberger Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Ochs_Sulzberger_Jr.), served as publisher until 2018. *The New York Times*'s current publisher is [A. G. Sulzberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._G._Sulzberger), Sulzberger Jr.'s son.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmber2017b-101] As of 2023, the *Times*'s executive editor is [Joseph Kahn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Kahn_\(journalist\))[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrynbaum2022a-120] and the paper's managing editors are [Marc Lacey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marc_Lacey) and [Carolyn Ryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carolyn_Ryan), having been appointed in June 2022.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrynbaumWindolf2022-121] *The New York Times*'s deputy managing editors are [Sam Dolnick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Dolnick),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBruell2023b-122] [Monica Drake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monica_Drake_\(journalist\)),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERobertsonKoblin2023-123] and [Steve Duenes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Duenes),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEManjoo2023-124] and the paper's assistant managing editors are Matthew Ericson,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGallogly2023-125] Jonathan Galinsky, Hannah Poferl, [Sam Sifton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Sifton), Karron Skog,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times2015b-126] and [Michael Slackman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Slackman).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFarago2022-127]

*The New York Times* is owned by [The New York Times Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Company), a publicly traded company. The New York Times Company, in addition to the *Times*, owns *[Wirecutter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wirecutter_\(website\))*, *[The Athletic](54936152.md)*, The New York Times Cooking, and [The New York Times Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Games), and acquired Serial Productions and Audm. The New York Times Company holds undisclosed minority investments in multiple other businesses, and formerly owned *[The Boston Globe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Boston_Globe)* and several radio and television stations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times2022b-128] The New York Times Company is majority-owned by the Ochs-Sulzberger family through elevated shares in the company's dual-class stock structure held largely in a trust, in effect since the 1950s;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENocera2012-129] as of 2022, the family holds 95% of The New York Times Company's [Class B shares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_B_share), allowing it to elect 70% of the company's board of directors.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarkerFontanella-Khan2022-130] [Class A shareholders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_A_share) have restrictive voting rights.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEllison2007-131] As of 2023, The New York Times Company's chief executive is [Meredith Kopit Levien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meredith_Kopit_Levien), the company's former chief operating officer who was appointed in September 2020.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELee2020-132]

### Journalists

See also: [List of *The New York Times* employees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_The_New_York_Times_employees)

As of March 2023, The New York Times Company employs 5,800 individuals,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatel2023-112] including 1,700 journalists according to deputy managing editor [Sam Dolnick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Dolnick).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFischer2023-133] Journalists for *The New York Times* may not run for public office, provide financial support to political candidates or causes, endorse candidates, or demonstrate public support for causes or movements.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times2022a-134] Journalists are subject to the guidelines established in "Ethical Journalism" and "Guidelines on Integrity".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalame2007-135] According to the former, *Times* journalists must abstain from using sources with a personal relationship to them and must not accept reimbursements or inducements from individuals who may be written about in *The New York Times*, with exceptions for gifts of nominal value.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times2018a-136] The latter requires attribution and exact quotations, though exceptions are made for linguistic anomalies. Staff writers are expected to ensure the veracity of all written claims, but may delegate researching obscure facts to the research desk.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times1999-137] In March 2021, the *Times* established a committee to avoid journalistic conflicts of interest with work written for *The New York Times*, following columnist [David Brooks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Brooks_\(commentator\))'s resignation from the [Aspen Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aspen_Institute) for his undisclosed work on the initiative Weave.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoore2021-138]
