### Editorial board

| *The New York Times* editorial board |
| --- |
| [Binyamin Appelbaum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binyamin_Appelbaum)Michelle CottleDavid FirestoneNick Fox[Mara Gay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mara_Gay)[Jeneen Interlandi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeneen_Interlandi)Lauren Kelley[Kathleen Kingsbury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathleen_Kingsbury)[Serge Schmemann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serge_Schmemann)[Brent Staples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brent_Staples)[Farah Stockman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farah_Stockman)Jyoti ThottamJesse Wegman |

*The New York Times* editorial board was established in 1896 by [Adolph Ochs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolph_Ochs). With the opinion department, the editorial board is independent of the newsroom.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBennet2020-184] Then-editor-in-chief [Charles Ransom Miller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Ransom_Miller) served as opinion editor from 1883 until his death in 1922.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times1922-185] [Rollo Ogden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rollo_Ogden) succeeded Miller until his death in 1937.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times1937-186] From 1937 to 1938, [John Huston Finley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Huston_Finley) served as opinion editor; in a prearranged plan, [Charles Merz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Merz) succeeded Finley.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times1938-187] Merz served in the position until his retirement in 1961.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcQuiston1977-188] [John Bertram Oakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Bertram_Oakes) served as opinion editor from 1961 to 1976, when then-publisher [Arthur Ochs Sulzberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Ochs_Sulzberger) appointed [Max Frankel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Frankel).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcFadden2001a-189] Frankel served in the position until 1986, when he was appointed as executive editor.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times1986-190] [Jack Rosenthal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_Rosenthal_\(journalist\)) was the opinion editor from 1986 to 1993.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoberts2017-191] [Howell Raines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howell_Raines) succeeded Rosenthal until 2001, when he was made executive editor.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcFadden2001b-192] [Gail Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gail_Collins) succeeded Raines until her resignation in 2006.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeelye2006-193] From 2007 to 2016, [Andrew Rosenthal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Rosenthal) was the opinion editor.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunlap2016b-194] [James Bennet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Bennet_\(journalist\)) succeeded Rosenthal until his resignation in 2020.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETracy2020-195] As of July 2024, the editorial board comprises thirteen opinion writers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times2018b-196] *The New York Times*'s opinion editor is [Kathleen Kingsbury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathleen_Kingsbury)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETracy2021-197] and the deputy opinion editor is Patrick Healy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times2015b-126]

*The New York Times*'s editorial board was initially opposed to liberal beliefs, opposing [women's suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_suffrage_in_the_United_States) in 1900 and 1914. The editorial board began to espouse progressive beliefs during Oakes's tenure, conflicting with the Ochs-Sulzberger family, of which Oakes was a member as Adolph Ochs's nephew; in 1976, Oakes publicly disagreed with Sulzberger's endorsement of [Daniel Patrick Moynihan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Patrick_Moynihan) over [Bella Abzug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bella_Abzug) in the [1976 Senate Democratic primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1976_United_States_Senate_election_in_New_York#Democratic_primary) in a letter sent from [Martha's Vineyard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martha's_Vineyard). Under Rosenthal, the editorial board took positions supporting [assault weapons legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assault_weapons_legislation_in_the_United_States) and the [legalization of marijuana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legalization_of_non-medical_cannabis_in_the_United_States), but publicly criticized the [Obama administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obama_administration) over its portrayal of terrorism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunlap2016b-194] In presidential elections, *The New York Times* has [endorsed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_presidential_election_endorsements_made_by_The_New_York_Times) a total of twelve Republican candidates and thirty-two Democratic candidates, and has endorsed the Democrat in every election since 1960.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdamsLouttitTaylor2016-198][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Editorial_Board2020-199][^cite_note-200] With the exception of [Wendell Willkie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wendell_Willkie), Republicans endorsed by the *Times* have won the presidency. In 2016, the editorial board issued an anti-endorsement against [Donald Trump](4848272.md) for the first time in its history.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliamson2016-201] In February 2020, the editorial board reduced its presence from several editorials each day to occasional editorials for events deemed particularly significant. Since August 2024, the board no longer endorses candidates in local or congressional races in New York.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERobertsonFandos2024-202]

### Unionization

Main article: [New York Times Guild](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Times_Guild)

Since 1940, editorial, media, and technology workers of *The New York Times* have been represented by the [New York Times Guild](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Times_Guild). The Times Guild, along with the Times Tech Guild, are represented by the [NewsGuild-CWA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NewsGuild-CWA).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFu2021-203] In 1940, [Arthur Hays Sulzberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Hays_Sulzberger) was called upon by the [National Labor Relations Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Labor_Relations_Board) amid accusations that he had discouraged Guild membership in the *Times*. Over the next few years, the Guild would ratify several contracts, expanding to editorial and news staff in 1942 and maintenance workers in 1943.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger1951496-204]

The New York Times Guild has walked out several times in its history, including for six and a half hours in 1981[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIzadi2022-205] and in 2017, when copy editors and reporters walked out at lunchtime in response to the elimination of the copy desk.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmber2017a-206] On December 7, 2022, the union held a one-day strike,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times2022c-207] the first interruption to *The New York Times* since 1978.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCreesh2022-208] The New York Times Guild reached an agreement in May 2023 to increase minimum salaries for employees and a retroactive bonus.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERobertson2023a-209] The Times Tech Guild is the largest [technology union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unionization_in_the_tech_sector) with [collective bargaining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_bargaining) rights in the United States.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERobertson2022-210] The guild held a second strike beginning on November 4, 2024, threatening the *Times*'s coverage of the [2024 United States presidential election](59568546.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWagner2024b-211]

## Content

### Circulation

As of June 2026, *The New York Times* has 13.35 million subscribers, with 12.8 million digital subscribers and 550,000 print subscribers. The New York Times Company is targeting 15 million subscribers by 2027.[^cite_note-circ826-2] The *Times*'s shift towards subscription-based revenue with the debut of an online paywall in 2011 contributed to subscription revenue exceeding advertising revenue the following year, furthered by the [2016 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election) and [Donald Trump](4848272.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKafkaMolla2017-212] In 2022, *[Vox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vox_\(website\))* wrote that *The New York Times*'s subscribers skew "older, richer, whiter, and more liberal"; to reflect the general population of the United States, the *Times* has attempted to alter its audience by acquiring *[The Athletic](54936152.md)*, investing in verticals such as *The New York Times Games*, and beginning a marketing campaign showing diverse subscribers to the *Times*. The New York Times Company chief executive [Meredith Kopit Levien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meredith_Kopit_Levien) stated that the average age of subscribers has remained constant.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKafka2022b-213]

### Newsletters

In October 2001, *The New York Times* began publishing *DealBook*, a financial newsletter edited by [Andrew Ross Sorkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Ross_Sorkin). The *Times* had intended to publish the newsletter in September, but delayed its debut following the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESorkin2011-214] A website for *DealBook* was established in March 2006.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDealBook2006-215] *The New York Times* began shifting towards *DealBook* as part of the newspaper's financial coverage in November 2010 with a renewed website and a presence in the *Times*'s print edition.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnett2010-216] In 2011, the *Times* began hosting the DealBook Summit, an annual conference hosted by Sorkin.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times2023-217] During the [COVID-19 pandemic](63136490.md), *The New York Times* hosted the DealBook Online Summit in 2020[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times2020-218] and 2021.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESorkin_et_al.2021-219] The 2022 DealBook Summit featured—among other speakers—former vice president [Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence) and Israeli prime minister [Benjamin Netanyahu](56472.md),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarantz2022-220] culminating in an interview with former [FTX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FTX) chief executive [Sam Bankman-Fried](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Bankman-Fried); FTX had [filed for bankruptcy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bankruptcy_of_FTX) several weeks prior.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKim2022-221] The 2023 DealBook Summit's speakers included vice president [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris), Israeli president [Isaac Herzog](4645588.md), and businessman [Elon Musk](909036.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times2023-217]

In June 2010, *The New York Times* licensed the political blog *[FiveThirtyEight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FiveThirtyEight)* in a three-year agreement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStelter2010-222] The blog, written by [Nate Silver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nate_Silver), had garnered attention during the [2008 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_United_States_presidential_election) for predicting the elections in forty-nine of fifty states. *FiveThirtyEight* appeared on nytimes.com in August.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESilver2010-223] According to Silver, several offers were made for the blog; Silver wrote that a merger of unequals must allow for editorial sovereignty and resources from the acquirer, comparing himself to [Groucho Marx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groucho_Marx).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarr2011-224] According to *[The New Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Republic)*, *FiveThirtyEight* drew as much as a fifth of the traffic to nytimes.com during the [2012 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_United_States_presidential_election).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETracy2012-225] In July 2013, *FiveThirtyEight* was sold to [ESPN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStelter2013-226] In an article following Silver's exit, public editor [Margaret Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Sullivan_\(journalist\)) wrote that he was disruptive to the *Times*'s culture for his perspective on probability-based predictions and scorn for polling—having stated that punditry is "fundamentally useless", comparing him to [Billy Beane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billy_Beane), who implemented [sabermetrics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabermetrics) in baseball. According to Sullivan, his work was criticized by several notable political journalists.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESullivan2013b-227]

*The New Republic* obtained a memo in November 2013 revealing then-Washington bureau chief [David Leonhardt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Leonhardt)'s ambitions to establish a data-driven newsletter with presidential historian [Michael Beschloss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Beschloss), graphic designer [Amanda Cox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amanda_Cox), economist [Justin Wolfers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justin_Wolfers), and *The New Republic* journalist [Nate Cohn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nate_Cohn).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETracy2013-228] By March, Leonhardt had amassed fifteen employees from within *The New York Times*; the newsletter's staff included individuals who had created the *Times*'s dialect quiz, [fourth down](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_down) analyzer, and a calculator for determining buying or renting a home.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcDuling2014-229] *[The Upshot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Upshot)* debuted in April 2014.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELeonhardt2014-230] *[Fast Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fast_Company)* reviewed an article about [Illinois](14849.md) Secure Choice—a state-funded retirement saving system—as "neither a terse news item, nor a formal financial advice column, nor a politically charged response to economic policy", citing its informal and neutral tone.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson2015-231] *The Upshot* developed "the needle" for the [2016 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election) and [2020 presidential elections](48410011.md), a thermometer dial displaying the probability of a candidate winning.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson2020-232] In January 2016, Cox was named editor of *The Upshot*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times_Company2016-233] Kevin Quealy was named editor in June 2022.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times_Company2022f-234]

### Political positions

*The New York Times* has said it is perceived as a liberal newspaper.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpayd2016-235] An analysis by [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) in October 2014 placed the *Times* readership as ideologically liberal based on a scale of 10 political values questions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlake2014-236] According to an internal readership poll conducted by *The New York Times* in 2019, 84% of readers identified as liberal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagourney2023464-237] *The New York Times* has struggled internally with how to balance its coverage, dismissing criticism from the left for "[sanewashing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanewashing)" right-wing viewpoints in its coverage of Donald Trump.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETani2024-238]

In covering Israel's [war on the Gaza Strip](74998519.md) that began in 2023, *The New York Times* instructed its reporters to restrict use of the terms '[Palestine](241405.md)', '[genocide](75668427.md)', and '[refugee camps](75393092.md)' to specific usages, with data analysis showing a pattern of articles emphasizing [Israeli civilians](74998317.md) killed by Palestinians over a much larger number of [Palestinian civilians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_war_crimes_in_the_Gaza_war) killed by Israelis.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEScahillGrim2024-239] The group [Writers Against the War on Gaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Writers_Against_the_War_on_Gaza) wrote in the blog [Mondoweiss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mondoweiss) that this has contrasted with *The New York Times* coverage of [Russia's invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine), in which Russia is considered a threat to U.S. foreign policy interests, while Israel is considered an ally.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWriters_Against_the_War_on_Gaza2024-240]

### Crossword

Main article: [*The New York Times* crossword puzzle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_crossword_puzzle)

In February 1942, [*The New York Times* crossword](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_crossword_puzzle) debuted in *[The New York Times Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Magazine)*; according to [Richard Shepard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Shepard), the [attack on Pearl Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attack_on_Pearl_Harbor) in December 1941 convinced then-publisher [Arthur Hays Sulzberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Hays_Sulzberger) of the necessity of a crossword, since he believed Americans would need something to occupy themselves with during blackouts caused by air raids.[^cite_note-Shepard-241]

### Cooking

*The New York Times* has published recipes since the 1850s and has had a separate food section since the 1940s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHesser2010a-242] In 1961, restaurant critic [Craig Claiborne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Craig_Claiborne) published *The New York Times Cookbook*,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHesser2010b1-243] an unauthorized cookbook that drew from the *Times*'s recipes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDisis2018-244] Since 2010, former food editor [Amanda Hesser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amanda_Hesser) has published *[The Essential New York Times Cookbook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Essential_New_York_Times_Cookbook)*, a compendium of recipes from *The New York Times*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEReuters2010-245] The *Innovation Report* in 2014 revealed that the *Times* had attempted to establish a cooking website since 1998, but faced difficulties with the absence of a defined data structure.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson2014-246] In September 2014, *The New York Times* introduced NYT Cooking, an application and website.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith2016-247] Edited by food editor [Sam Sifton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Sifton),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDisis2018-244] the *Times*'s cooking website features 21,000 recipes as of 2022.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGapper2022-248] NYT Cooking features videos as part of an effort by Sifton to hire two former [*Tasty*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tasty_\(web_series\)) employees from [BuzzFeed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BuzzFeed).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDisis2018-244] In August 2023, NYT Cooking added personalized recommendations through the [cosine similarity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosine_similarity) of text embeddings of recipe titles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFittsEddy2023-249] The website also features no-recipe recipes, a concept proposed by Sifton.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeinstein2019-250]

In May 2016, The New York Times Company announced a partnership with startup Chef'd to form a meal delivery service that would deliver ingredients from The New York Times Cooking recipes to subscribers;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOpam2016-251] Chef'd shut down in July 2018 after failing to accrue capital and secure financing.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaddon2018-252] *[The Hollywood Reporter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hollywood_Reporter)* reported in September 2022 that the *Times* would expand its delivery options to US\$95 cooking kits curated by chefs such as [Nina Compton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nina_Compton), Chintan Pandya, and Naoko Takei Moore. That month, the staff of NYT Cooking went on tour with Compton, Pandya, and Moore in Los Angeles, [New Orleans](53842.md), and New York City, culminating in a food festival.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChan2022-253] In addition, *The New York Times* offered its own [wine club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wine_club) originally operated by the Global Wine Company. The New York Times Wine Club was established in August 2009, during a dramatic decrease in advertising revenue.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times2009-254] By 2021, the wine club was managed by [Lot18](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lot18), a company that provides proprietary labels. Lot18 managed the [Williams Sonoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Williams_Sonoma) Wine Club and its own wine club Tasting Room.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsimov2021-255]

### Archives

Main article: [*The New York Times* Archival Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Archival_Library)

*The New York Times* archives its articles in [a basement annex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Archival_Library) beneath its building known as "the morgue", a venture started by managing editor [Carr Van Anda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carr_Van_Anda) in 1907. The morgue comprises news clippings, a pictures library, and the *Times*'s book and periodicals library. As of 2014, it is the largest library of any media company, dating back to 1851.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllen2014-256] In November 2018, *The New York Times* partnered with Google to digitize the Archival Library.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVincent2018-257] Additionally, *The New York Times* has maintained a virtual microfilm reader known as TimesMachine since 2014. The service launched with archives from 1851 to 1980; in 2016, TimesMachine expanded to include archives from 1981 to 2002. The *Times* built a pipeline to take in [TIFF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TIFF) images, article metadata in [XML](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/XML) and an [INI file](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INI_file) of [Cartesian geometry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cartesian_geometry) describing the boundaries of the page, and convert it into a [PNG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PNG) of image tiles and [JSON](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JSON) containing the information in the XML and INI files. The image tiles are generated using [GDAL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GDAL) and displayed using [Leaflet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leaflet_\(software\)), using data from a [content delivery network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Content_delivery_network). The *Times* ran [optical character recognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optical_character_recognition) on the articles using [Tesseract](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tesseract_\(software\)) and [shingled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N-gram) and [fuzzy string matched](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Approximate_string_matching) the result.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECotlerSandhaus2016-258]

### Content management system

*The New York Times* uses a proprietary[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChayka2019-259] [content management system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Content_management_system) known as Scoop for its online content and the [Microsoft Word](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microsoft_Word)-based content management system [CCI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CCI_Europe) for its print content. Scoop was developed in 2008 to serve as a secondary content management system for editors working in CCI to publish their content on the *Times*'s website; as part of *The New York Times*'s online endeavors, editors now write their content in Scoop and send their work to CCI for print publication. Since its introduction, Scoop has superseded several processes within the *Times*, including print edition planning and collaboration, and features tools such as multimedia integration, notifications, content tagging, and drafts. *The New York Times* uses private articles for high-profile opinion pieces, such as those written by Russian president [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) and actress [Angelina Jolie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angelina_Jolie), and for high-level investigations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVnenchak2014-260] In January 2012, the *Times* released Integrated Content Editor (ICE), a revision tracking tool for [WordPress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WordPress) and [TinyMCE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TinyMCE). ICE is integrated within the *Times*'s workflow by providing a unified text editor for print and online editors, reducing the divide between print and online operations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMyers2012-261]

By 2017,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller2017-262] *The New York Times* began developing a new authoring tool to its content management system known as Oak, in an attempt to further the *Times*'s visual efforts in articles and reduce the discrepancy between the mediums in print and online articles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdmonds2018-263] The system reduces the input of editors and supports additional visual mediums in an editor that resembles the appearance of the article.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller2017-262] Oak is based on ProseMirror, a [JavaScript](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JavaScript) rich-text editor toolkit, and retains the revision tracking and commenting functionalities of *The New York Times*'s previous systems. Additionally, Oak supports predefined article headers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECiocca2018-264] In 2019, Oak was updated to support collaborative editing using [Firebase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Firebase) to update editors's cursor status. Several Google Cloud Functions and Google Cloud Tasks allow articles to be previewed as they will be printed, and the *Times*'s primary [MySQL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MySQL) database is regularly updated to update editors on the article status.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECioccaSisson2019-265]

## Style and design

### Style guide

Main article: [*The New York Times Manual of Style and Usage*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Manual_of_Style_and_Usage)

Since 1895, *The New York Times* has maintained a [manual of style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Manual_of_Style_and_Usage) in several forms. *The New York Times Manual of Style and Usage* was published on the *Times*'s [intranet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intranet) in 1999.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKallaur2016-266]

*The New York Times* uses [honorifics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honorific) when referring to individuals. With the *[AP Stylebook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AP_Stylebook)*'s removal of honorifics in 2000 and *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)*'s omission of courtesy titles in May 2023, the *Times* is the only national newspaper that continues to use honorifics. According to former copy editor Merrill Perlman, *The New York Times* continues to use honorifics as a "sign of civility".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBranigin2023-267] The *Times*'s use of courtesy titles led to an apocryphal rumor that the paper had referred to singer [Meat Loaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meat_Loaf) as "Mr. Loaf".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStevens2022-268] Several exceptions have been made; the former sports section and *[The New York Times Book Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Book_Review)* do not use honorifics.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPadnaniChambers2020-269] A leaked memo following the [killing of Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Osama_bin_Laden) in May 2011 revealed that editors were given a last-minute instruction to omit the honorific from [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden)'s name, consistent with deceased figures of historic significance, such as [Adolf Hitler](2731583.md), [Napoleon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon), and [Vladimir Lenin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Lenin).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBonner2011-270] *The New York Times* uses academic and military titles for individuals prominently serving in that position.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorbett2017-271] In 1986, the *Times* began to use Ms,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPadnaniChambers2020-269] and introduced the gender-neutral title [Mx.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mx.) in 2015.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorbett2015-272] *The New York Times* uses initials when a subject has expressed a preference, such as [Donald Trump](4848272.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBagli2016-273]

*The New York Times* maintains a strict but not absolute obscenity policy, including phrases. In a review of the Canadian [hardcore punk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardcore_punk) band [Fucked Up](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fucked_Up), music critic [Kelefa Sanneh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kelefa_Sanneh) wrote that the band's name—entirely rendered in asterisks—would not be printed in the *Times* "unless an American president, or someone similar, says it by mistake";[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanneh2007-274] *The New York Times* did not repeat then-vice president [Dick Cheney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick_Cheney)'s use of "fuck" against then-senator [Patrick Leahy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrick_Leahy) in 2004[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStolberg2004-275] or then-vice president [Joe Biden](145422.md)'s remarks that the passage of the [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act) in 2010 was a "big fucking deal".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHerszenhorn2010-276] The *Times*'s profanity policy has been tested by former president Donald Trump. *The New York Times* published Trump's [*Access Hollywood* tape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_Access_Hollywood_tape) in October 2016, containing the words "fuck", "pussy", "bitch", and "tits", the first time the publication had published an expletive on its front page,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEskin2016-277] and repeated an explicit phrase for fellatio stated by then-[White House](33057.md) communications director [Anthony Scaramucci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Scaramucci) in July 2017.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFrance2017-278] *The New York Times* omitted Trump's use of the phrase "[shithole countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shithole_countries)" from its headline in favor of "vulgar language" in January 2018.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrynbaum2018-279] The *Times* banned certain words, such as "bitch", "whore", and "sluts", from *[Wordle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wordle)* in 2022.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiaz2022a-280]

### Headlines

Journalists for *The New York Times* do not write their own headlines, but rather copy editors who specifically write headlines. The *Times*'s guidelines insist headline editors get to the main point of an article but avoid giving away endings, if present. Other guidelines include using slang "sparingly", avoiding [tabloid headlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabloid_journalism), not ending a line on a preposition, article, or adjective, and chiefly, not to pun. *[The New York Times Manual of Style and Usage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Manual_of_Style_and_Usage)* states that wordplay, such as "Rubber Industry Bounces Back", is to be tested on a colleague as a [canary is to be tested in a coal mine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canary_in_a_coal_mine); "when no song bursts forth, start rewriting".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHiltner2017b-281] *The New York Times* has amended headlines due to controversy. In 2019, following two back-to-back mass shootings in [El Paso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_El_Paso_Walmart_shooting) and [Dayton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Dayton_shooting), the *Times* used the headline, "Trump Urges Unity vs. Racism", to describe then-president [Donald Trump](4848272.md)'s words after the shootings. After criticism from *[FiveThirtyEight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FiveThirtyEight)* founder [Nate Silver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nate_Silver), the headline was changed to, "Assailing Hate But Not Guns".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChiu2019-282]

Online, *The New York Times*'s headlines do not face the same length restrictions as headlines that appear in print; print headlines must fit within a column, often six words. Additionally, headlines must "break" properly, containing a complete thought on each line without splitting up prepositions and adverbs. Writers may edit a headline to fit an article more aptly if further developments occur. The *Times* uses [A/B testing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A/B_testing) for articles on the front page, placing two headlines against each other. At the end of the test, the headlines that receives more traffic is chosen.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBulik2016-283] The alteration of a headline regarding intercepted Russian data used in the [Mueller special counsel investigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mueller_special_counsel_investigation) was noted by Trump in a March 2017 interview with *[Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\))*, in which he claimed that the headline used the word "wiretapped" in the print version of the paper on January 20, while the digital article on January 19 omitted the word. The headline was intentionally changed in the print version to use "wiretapped" in order to fit within the print guidelines.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESymonds2017-284]

### Nameplate

The nameplate of *The New York Times* has been unaltered since 1967. In creating the initial nameplate, [Henry Jarvis Raymond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Jarvis_Raymond) took as his model the British newspaper *[The Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times)*, which used a [Blackletter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackletter) style called [Textura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textura), popularized following the [fall of the Western Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Western_Roman_Empire) and regional variations of [Alcuin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcuin)'s script, as well as a period. With the change to *The New-York Times* on September 14, 1857, the nameplate followed. Under [George Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Jones_\(publisher\)), the [terminals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terminal_\(typography\)) of the "N", "r", and "s" were intentionally exaggerated into swashes. The nameplate in the January 15, 1894, issue trimmed the terminals once more, smoothed the edges, and turned the stem supporting the "T" into an ornament. The hyphen was dropped on December 1, 1896, after [Adolph Ochs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolph_Ochs) purchased the paper. The [descender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Descender) of the "h" was shortened on December 30, 1914. The largest change to the nameplate was introduced on February 21, 1967, when type designer [Ed Benguiat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ed_Benguiat) redesigned the logo, most prominently turning the arrow ornament into a diamond. Notoriously, the new logo dropped the [period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_stop) that had followed the word *Times* up until that point; one reader compared the omission of the period to "performing plastic surgery on [Helen of Troy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helen_of_Troy)." Picture editor John Radosta worked with a [New York University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_University) professor to determine that dropping the period saved the paper US\$41.28 (equivalent to \$398.59 in 2025).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunlap2017b-285]

## Print edition

### Design and layout

As of December 2023, *The New York Times* has printed sixty thousand issues, a statistic represented in the paper's masthead to the right of the volume number, the *Times*'s years in publication written in [Roman numerals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_numerals).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunlap2023c-286] The volume and issues are separated by four dots representing the edition number of that issue; on the day of the 2000 presidential election, the *Times* was revised four separate times, necessitating the use of an [em dash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Em_dash) in place of an ellipsis.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunlap2014c-287] The em dash issue was printed hundreds times over before being replaced by the one-dot issue. Despite efforts by newsroom employees to recycle copies sent to *The New York Times*'s office, several copies were kept, including one put on display at the Museum at The Times.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunlap2023a-288] From February 7, 1898, to December 31, 1999, the *Times*'s issue number was incorrect by five hundred issues, an error suspected by *[The Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Atlantic)* to be the result of a careless front page type editor. The misreporting was noticed by news editor Aaron Donovan, who was calculating the number of issues in a spreadsheet and noticed the discrepancy. *The New York Times* celebrated fifty thousand issues on March 14, 1995, an observance that should have occurred on July 26, 1996.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERosen2014-289]

*The New York Times* has reduced the physical size of its print edition while retaining its [broadsheet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadsheet) format. *The New-York Daily Times* debuted at 18 inches (460 mm) across. By the 1950s, the *Times* was being printed at 16 inches (410 mm) across. In 1953, an increase in paper costs to US\$10 (equivalent to \$120.34 in 2025) a ton increased newsprint costs to US\$21.7 million (equivalent to \$326,110,074.63 in 2025). On December 28, 1953, the pages were reduced to 15.5 inches (390 mm). On February 14, 1955, a further reduction to 15 inches (380 mm) occurred, followed by 14.5 and 13.5 inches (370 and 340 mm). On August 6, 2007, the largest cut occurred when the pages were reduced to 12 inches (300 mm),[^cite_note-291] a decision that other broadsheets had previously considered. Then-executive editor [Bill Keller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Keller) stated that a narrower paper would be more beneficial to the reader but acknowledged a net loss in article space of 5%.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunlap2016f-290] In 1985, The New York Times Company established a minority stake in a US\$21.7 million (equivalent to \$326,110,074.63 in 2025) newsprint plant in [Clermont, Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clermont,_Capitale-Nationale,_Quebec) through [Donahue Malbaie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resolute_Forest_Products).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEReuters1985-292] The company sold its equity interest in Donahue Malbaie in 2017.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times_Company2020a22-293]

*The New York Times* often uses large, bolded headlines for major events. For the print version of the *Times*, these headlines are written by one copy editor, reviewed by two other copy editors, approved by the masthead editors, and polished by other print editors. The process is completed before 8 p.m., but it may be repeated if further development occur, as did take place during the [2020 presidential election](48410011.md). On the day [Joe Biden](145422.md) was declared the winner, *The New York Times* utilized a "hammer headline" reading, "Biden Beats Trump", in all caps and bolded. A dozen journalists discussed several potential headlines, such as "It's Biden" or "Biden's Moment", and prepared for a [Donald Trump](4848272.md) victory, in which they would use "Trump Prevails".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEErnstVecsey2020-294] During Trump's [first impeachment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_impeachment_of_Donald_Trump), the *Times* drafted the hammer headline, "Trump Impeached". *The New York Times* altered the [ligatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ligature_\(writing\)) between the E and the A, as not doing so would leave a noticeable gap due to the stem of the A sloping away from the E. The *Times* reused the tight [kerning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerning) for "Biden Beats Trump" and Trump's [second impeachment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_impeachment_of_Donald_Trump), which simply read, "Impeached".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESondern2021-295]

In cases where two major events occur on the same day or immediately after each other, *The New York Times* has used a "paddle wheel" headline, where both headlines are used but split by a line. The term dates back to August 8, 1959, when it was revealed that the United States was monitoring Soviet missile firings and when [Explorer 6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Explorer_6)—shaped like a [paddle wheel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paddle_wheel)—launched. Since then, the paddle wheel has been used several times, including on January 21, 1981, when [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) was [sworn in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_inauguration_of_Ronald_Reagan) minutes before [Iran](14653.md) released fifty-two American hostages, ending the [Iran hostage crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_hostage_crisis). At the time, most newspapers favored the end of the hostage crisis, but the *Times* placed the inauguration above the crisis. Other occasions in which the paddle wheel has been used include on July 26, 2000, when the [2000 Camp David Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Camp_David_Summit) ended without an agreement and when Bush announced that [Dick Cheney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick_Cheney) would be his running mate, and on June 24, 2016, when the [United Kingdom European Union membership referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_Kingdom_European_Union_membership_referendum) passed, beginning [Brexit](41688778.md), and when the [Supreme Court](31737.md) deadlocked in *[United States v. Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Texas_\(2016\))*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunlap2016e-296]

*The New York Times* has run editorials from its editorial board on the front page twice. On June 13, 1920, the *Times* ran an editorial opposing [Warren G. Harding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warren_G._Harding), who was nominated during that year's [Republican Party presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1920_Republican_Party_presidential_primaries).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldfarb2015-297] Amid growing acceptance to run editorials on the front pages[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETompkins2015-298] from publications such as the *[Detroit Free Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Free_Press)*, *[The Patriot-News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Patriot-News)*, *[The Arizona Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Arizona_Republic)*, and *[The Indianapolis Star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Indianapolis_Star)*, *The New York Times* ran an editorial on its front page on December 5, 2015, following [a terrorist attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_San_Bernardino_attack) in [San Bernardino, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Bernardino,_California), in which fourteen people were killed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENelson2015-299] The editorial advocates for the prohibition of "slightly modified combat rifles" used in the San Bernardino shooting and "certain kinds of ammunition".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldfarb2015-297] Conservative figures, including [Texas](29810.md) senator [Ted Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Cruz), *[The Weekly Standard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Weekly_Standard)* editor [Bill Kristol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Kristol), *[Fox & Friends](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_&_Friends)* co-anchor [Steve Doocy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Doocy), and then-[New Jersey](21648.md) governor [Chris Christie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Christie) criticized the *Times*. Talk radio host [Erick Erickson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erick_Erickson) acquired an issue of *The New York Times* to fire several rounds into the paper, posting a picture online.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKludt2015-300]

### Printing process



*The New York Times*'s distribution center in [College Point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_Point), Queens

Since 1997,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPeterson1997-301] *The New York Times*'s primary distribution center is located in [College Point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_Point), Queens. The facility is 300,000 ft<sup>2</sup> (28,000 m<sup>2</sup>) and employs 170 people as of 2017. The College Point distribution center prints 300,000 to 800,000 newspapers daily. On most occasions, presses start before 11 p.m. and finish before 3 a.m. A robotic crane grabs a roll of newsprint and several rollers ensure ink can be printed on paper. The final newspapers are wrapped in plastic and shipped out.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELeeKoppelQuick2017-302] As of 2018, the College Point facility accounted for 41% of production. Other copies are printed at 26 other publications, such as *[The Atlanta Journal-Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Atlanta_Journal-Constitution)*, *[The Dallas Morning News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Dallas_Morning_News)*, *[The Santa Fe New Mexican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Santa_Fe_New_Mexican)*, and the (Louisville) *[Courier Journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Courier_Journal)*. With the [decline of newspapers](22025015.md), particularly regional publications, the *Times* must travel further; for example, newspapers for Hawaii are flown from San Francisco on [United Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Airlines), and Sunday papers are flown from Los Angeles on [Hawaiian Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_Airlines). Computer glitches, mechanical issues, and weather phenomena affect circulation but do not stop the paper from reaching customers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_Syckle2018-303] The College Point facility prints over two dozen other papers, including *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)* and *[USA Today](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USA_Today)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunlap2023b-304]

*The New York Times* has halted its printing process several times to account for major developments. The first printing stoppage occurred on March 31, 1968, when then-president [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson) announced that he would not seek a second term. Other press stoppages include May 19, 1994, for the death of former first lady [Jacqueline Kennedy Onassis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacqueline_Kennedy_Onassis), and July 17, 1996, for [Trans World Airlines Flight 800](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans_World_Airlines_Flight_800). The [2000 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_United_States_presidential_election) necessitated two press stoppages. [Al Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Gore) appeared to concede on November 8, forcing then-executive editor [Joseph Lelyveld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Lelyveld) to stop the *Times*'s presses to print a new headline, "Bush Appears to Defeat Gore", with a story that stated [George W. Bush](3414021.md) was elected president. However, Gore held off his concession speech over doubts over [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_United_States_presidential_election_in_Florida). Lelyveld reran the headline, "Bush and Gore Vie for an Edge". Since 2000, three printing stoppages have been issued: for the death of [William Rehnquist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Rehnquist) on September 3, 2005, for the [killing of Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Osama_bin_Laden) on May 1, 2011, and for the passage of the [Marriage Equality Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marriage_Equality_Act_\(New_York\)) in the [New York State Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_State_Assembly) and subsequent signage by then-governor [Andrew Cuomo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Cuomo) on June 24, 2011.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunlap2016d-305]

## Online platforms

Main article: [Online platforms of *The New York Times*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Online_platforms_of_The_New_York_Times)

### Website

The *New York Times* website is hosted at nytimes.com. It has undergone several major redesigns and infrastructure developments since its debut. In April 2006, *The New York Times* redesigned its website with an emphasis on multimedia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEApcar2006-306] In preparation for [Super Tuesday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Tuesday,_2008) in February 2008, the *Times* developed a live election system using the [Associated Press](18935732.md)'s [File Transfer Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File_Transfer_Protocol) (FTP) service and a [Ruby on Rails](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruby_on_Rails) application; nytimes.com experienced its largest traffic on Super Tuesday and the day after.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWillis2008-307]

### Applications

The NYTimes application debuted with the introduction of the [App Store](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/App_Store_\(Apple\)) on July 10, 2008. *[Engadget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Engadget)*'s Scott McNulty wrote critically of the app, negatively comparing it to *The New York Times*'s mobile website.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcNulty2008-308] An [iPad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPad) version with select articles was released on April 3, 2010, with the release of the [first-generation iPad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPad_\(1st_generation\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChittum2010-309] In October, *The New York Times* expanded NYT Editors' Choice to include the paper's full articles. NYT for iPad was free until 2011.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESorrel2010-310] The *Times* applications on [iPhone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPhone) and iPad began offering in-app subscriptions in July 2011.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchramm2011-311] The *Times* released a [web application](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Web_application) for iPad—featuring a format summarizing trending headlines on Twitter[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeater2012a-312]—and a [Windows 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Windows_8) application in October 2012.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeater2012b-313]

Efforts to ensure profitability through an online magazine and a "Need to Know" subscription emerged in *[Adweek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adweek)* in July 2013.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTED'Orazio2013-314] In March 2014, *The New York Times* announced three applications—NYT Now, an application that offers pertinent news in a blog format, and two unnamed applications, later known as NYT Opinion[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeyer2014b-315] and NYT Cooking[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson2014-246]—to diversify its product laterals.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams2014-316]

### Podcasts

> *[The Daily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Daily_\(podcast\))* is the modern front page of *The New York Times*.

— [Sam Dolnick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Dolnick), speaking to *[Intelligencer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelligencer_\(website\))* in January 2020

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchneier2020-317]

*The New York Times* manages several podcasts, including multiple podcasts with Serial Productions. The *Times*'s longest-running podcast is *The Book Review Podcast*,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBisley2017-318] debuting as *Inside [The New York Times Book Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Book_Review)* in April 2006.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPaul2015-319]

*The New York Times*'s defining podcast is *[The Daily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Daily_\(podcast\))*,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchneier2020-317] a daily news podcast hosted by [Michael Barbaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Barbaro) which debuted on February 1, 2017.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarbaro2017-320] Between March 2022 and March 2025, the approximately 30 minute programme was co-hosted with [Sabrina Tavernise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabrina_Tavernise).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEQuah2022-321] Beginning in April 2025 Barbaro was joined by two new regular co-hosts, [Natalie Kitroeff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natalie_Kitroeff?action=edit\&redlink=1) and [Rachel Abrams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rachel_Abrams).[^cite_note-322]

*The Interview* was launched in 2024 and is hosted weekly by [David Marchese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Marchese) and [Lulu Garcia-Navarro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lulu_Garcia-Navarro). Episodes typically last 40 to 50 minutes. Condensed versions of the interviews are published simultaneously in *[The New York Times Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Magazine)*.[^cite_note-323] Guests have included politicians, actors, influential experts, media figures and high-profile writers.

In October 2021, *The New York Times* began testing "New York Times Audio", an application featuring podcasts from the *Times*, audio versions of articles—including from other publications through Audm, and archives from *[This American Life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/This_American_Life)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith2021-324] The application debuted in May 2023 exclusively on [iOS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IOS) for *Times* subscribers. New York Times Audio includes exclusive podcasts such as *The Headlines*, a daily news recap, and *Shorts*, short audio stories under ten minutes. In addition, a "Reporter Reads" section features *Times* journalists reading their articles and providing commentary.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhalid2023-325]

### Games

Main article: [*The New York Times Games*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Games)

*The New York Times* has used video games as part of its journalistic efforts, among the first publications to do so,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGómez-Garcíade_la_Hera_Conde-Pumpido2023451-326] contributing to an increase in Internet traffic;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUsher2014150-327] the publication has also developed its own video games. In 2014, *[The New York Times Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Magazine)* introduced *[Spelling Bee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Spelling_Bee)*, a [word game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Word_game) in which players guess words from a set of letters in a [honeycomb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honeycomb) and are awarded points for the length of the word and receive extra points if the word is a [pangram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pangram).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmlen2020-328] The game was proposed by [Will Shortz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Will_Shortz), created by [Frank Longo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Longo), and has been maintained by [Sam Ezersky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Ezersky). In May 2018, *Spelling Bee* was published on nytimes.com, furthering its popularity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELippman2020-329] In February 2019, the *Times* introduced *Letter Boxed*, in which players form words from letters placed on the edges of a square box,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESarkar2019-330] followed in June 2019 by *Tiles*, a [matching game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matching_game) in which players form sequences of tile pairings, and *Vertex*, in which players connect vertices to assemble an image.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times_Company2023d-331] In July 2023, *The New York Times* introduced *[Connections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connections_\(2023_video_game\))*, in which players identify groups of words that are connected by a common property.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorris2023-332] In April, the *Times* introduced *Digits*, a game that required using [operations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_\(mathematics\)) on different values to reach a set number; *Digits* was shut down in August.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPeters2023c-333] In March 2024, *The New York Times* released *[Strands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Strands)*, a themed [word search](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Word_search).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevine2024-334]

In January 2022, The New York Times Company acquired *[Wordle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wordle)*, a word game developed by [Josh Wardle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josh_Wardle) in 2021, at a valuation in the "low-seven figures".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPisani2022-335] The acquisition was proposed by David Perpich, a member of the Sulzberger family who proposed the purchase to Knight[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein2023d-336] over [Slack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slack_\(software\)) after reading about the game.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBruell2023a-337] *[The Washington Post](102226.md)* purportedly considered acquiring *Wordle*, according to *[Vanity Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vanity_Fair_\(magazine\))*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein2023d-336] At the 2022 [Game Developers Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Game_Developers_Conference), Wardle stated that he was overwhelmed by the volume of *Wordle* facsimiles and overzealous monetization practices in other games.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMachkovech2022-338] Concerns over *The New York Times* monetizing *Wordle* by implementing a paywall mounted;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMukherjeeDatta2022-339] *Wordle* is a client-side [browser game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Browser_game) and can be played offline by downloading its webpage.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHollister2022-340] *Wordle* moved to the *Times*'s servers and website in February.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarpenter2022-341] The game was added to the NYT Games application in August,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHicks2022-342] necessitating it be rewritten in the [JavaScript](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JavaScript) library [React](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/React_\(software\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOrland2023-343] In November, *The New York Times* announced that [Tracy Bennett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tracy_Bennett) would be the *Wordle*'s editor.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOrland2022-344]

## Other publications

### *The New York Times Magazine*

Main article: [*The New York Times Magazine*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Magazine)

*[The New York Times Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Magazine)* and *[The Boston Globe Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Boston_Globe_Magazine)* are the only weekly Sunday magazines following *[The Washington Post Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Washington_Post_Magazine)*'s cancellation in December 2022.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEllison2022-345]

### *The New York Times International Edition*

Main article: [*The New York Times International Edition*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_International_Edition)

#### *The New York Times in Spanish*

In February 2016, *The New York Times* introduced a Spanish website, *The New York Times en Español*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPolgreen2016-346] The website, intended to be read on mobile devices, would contain translated articles from the *Times* and reporting from journalists based in [Mexico City](18987.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYu2016-347] The *Times en Español*'s style editor is Paulina Chavira, who has advocated for pluralistic Spanish to accommodate the variety of nationalities in the newsroom's journalists and wrote a stylebook for *The New York Times en Español*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEArchibold2018-348]

Articles the *Times* intends to publish in Spanish are sent to a translation agency and adapted for Spanish writing conventions; the present progressive tense may be used for forthcoming events in English, but other tenses are preferable in Spanish. The *Times en Español* consults the [Real Academia Española](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_Academia_Espa%C3%B1ola) and [Fundéu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fund%C3%A9u) and frequently modifies the use of diacritics—such as using an acute accent for the [Cártel de Sinaloa](16881931.md) but not the [Cartel de Medellín](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medell%C3%ADn_Cartel)—and using the gender-neutral pronoun [*elle*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elle_\(Spanish_pronoun\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBudasoff2019-349] Headlines in *The New York Times en Español* are not capitalized. The *Times en Español* publishes *El Times*, a newsletter led by Elda Cantú intended for all Spanish speakers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcGinley2023-350] In September 2019, *The New York Times* ended *The New York Times en Español*'s separate operations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENarea2019-351] A study published in *The Translator* in 2023 found that the *Times en Español* engaged in [tabloidization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabloidization).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEValdeón2023-352]

#### *The New York Times in Chinese*

In June 2012, *The New York Times* introduced a Chinese website, 纽约时报中文, in response to Chinese editions created by *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)* and the *[Financial Times](136566.md)*. Conscious to [censorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship_in_China), the *Times* established servers outside of China and affirmed that the website would uphold the paper's journalistic standards; the [government of China](23239.md) had previously blocked articles from nytimes.com through the [Great Firewall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Firewall_of_China),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaughney2012-353] and the website was blocked in China until August 2001 after then-general secretary [Jiang Zemin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiang_Zemin) met with journalists from *The New York Times*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBradsher2012-354] Then-foreign editor [Joseph Kahn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Kahn_\(journalist\)) assisted in the establishment of cn.nytimes.com, an effort that contributed to his appointment as executive editor in April 2022.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrynbaum2022b-355]

In October 2012, 纽约时报中文 published an article detailing the wealth of then-premier [Wen Jiabao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wen_Jiabao)'s family. In response, the government of China blocked access to nytimes.com and cn.nytimes.com and references to the *Times* and Wen were censored on microblogging service [Sina Weibo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sina_Weibo).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBradsher2012-354] In March 2015, a [mirror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirror_site) of 纽约时报中文 and the website for [GreatFire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GreatFire) were the targets for a government-sanctioned [distributed denial of service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distributed_denial_of_service) attack on [GitHub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GitHub) in March 2015, disabling access to the service for several days.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoodin2015-356] Chinese authorities requested the removal of *The New York Times*'s news applications from the [App Store](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/App_Store_\(Apple\)) in December 2016.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBennerWee2017-357]

## Awards and recognition

### Awards

Main articles: [List of awards won by *The New York Times*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_awards_won_by_The_New_York_Times) and [List of Pulitzer Prizes awarded to *The New York Times*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Pulitzer_Prizes_awarded_to_The_New_York_Times)

As of 2023, *The New York Times* has [received](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Pulitzer_Prizes_awarded_to_The_New_York_Times) 137 [Pulitzer Prizes](24230.md),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAx2023-358] the most of any publication.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFolkenflik2022-359]

### Recognition

*The New York Times* is considered a [newspaper of record](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newspaper_of_record) in the United States.[^cite_note-363] The *Times* is the largest metropolitan newspaper in the United States;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECommunications_and_Digital_Committee2008123-364] as of 2022, *The New York Times* is the [second-largest newspaper by print circulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_newspapers_in_the_United_States) in the United States behind *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Visé2022-365]

A study published in *[Science, Technology, & Human Values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science,_Technology,_&_Human_Values)* in 2013 found that *The New York Times* received more citations in academic journals than the *[American Sociological Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Sociological_Review)*, *[Research Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_Policy_\(journal\))*, or the *[Harvard Law Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard_Law_Review)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHicksWang2013-366] With sixteen million unique records, the *Times* is the third-most referenced source in [Common Crawl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Crawl), a collection of online material used in datasets such as [GPT-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GPT-3), behind Wikipedia and a United States patent database.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETimmer2023-367]

*[The New Yorker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Yorker)*'s Max Norman wrote in March 2023 that the *Times* has shaped mainstream English usage.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENorman2023-368]  In a January 2018 article for *[The Washington Post](102226.md)*, [Margaret Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Sullivan_\(journalist\)) stated that *The New York Times* affects the "whole media and political ecosystem".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESullivan2018-369]

*The New York Times*'s success has led to concerns over media consolidation, particularly amid the [decline of newspapers](22025015.md). In 2006, economists Lisa George and [Joel Waldfogel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joel_Waldfogel) examined the consequences of the *Times*'s national distribution strategy and audience with circulation of local newspapers, finding that local circulation decreased among college-educated readers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgeWaldfogel2006446-370] The effect of *The New York Times* in this manner was observed in *[The Forum of Fargo-Moorhead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Forum_of_Fargo-Moorhead)*, the newspaper of record for [Fargo, North Dakota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fargo,_North_Dakota).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgeWaldfogel2006435-371] *[Axios](52436164.md)* founder [Jim VandeHei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_VandeHei) opined that the *Times* is "going to basically be a monopoly" in an opinion piece written by then-media columnist and former *[BuzzFeed News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BuzzFeed_News)* editor-in-chief [Ben Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Smith_\(journalist\)); in the article, Smith cites the strength of *The New York Times*'s journalistic workforce, broadening content, and the expropriation of *[Gawker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gawker)* editor-in-chief [Choire Sicha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Choire_Sicha), *[Recode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recode)* editor-in-chief [Kara Swisher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kara_Swisher), and *[Quartz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quartz_\(publication\))* editor-in-chief Kevin Delaney. Smith compared the *Times* to the [New York Yankees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Yankees) during their [1927 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1927_New_York_Yankees_season) containing [Murderers' Row](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murderers'_Row).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith2020a-372]

## Controversies

Main article: [List of The New York Times controversies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_The_New_York_Times_controversies)

### Israeli–Palestinian conflict

Since 2003, studies analyzing coverage of the [Israeli–Palestinian conflict](46216.md) in the *New York Times* have demonstrated a bias [against Palestinians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Palestinianism) and in favor of Israel.[^cite_note-378]

#### Gaza war

Further information: [Media coverage of the Gaza war § The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_coverage_of_the_Gaza_war#The_New_York_Times), [Screams Without Words](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Screams_Without_Words), and [The Silence That Meets the Rape of Palestinians](83232037.md)

*The New York Times* has received criticism for its coverage of the [Gaza war](74998519.md) and [genocide](75668427.md).[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFolkenflik2024-380] In September 2026 shareholders filed a lawsuit requesting that the Times release internal records regarding decisions about their Gaza coverage, amid claims that the Times violated its own editorial policies in its coverage. In particular it claims that bias concerns were not addressed and not brought to the attention of the leadership, and when it was, appropriate action was not taken. The suit is in partial response to the lack of action by the TImes for a response to the [National Jewish Advocacy Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Jewish_Advocacy_Center) (NJAC), request on May 29 to the May 11 [Nicholas Kristof](435304.md) column titled “The Silence That Meets the Rape of Palestinians" .[^cite_note-381][^cite_note-382][^cite_note-383]

In April 2024, *[The Intercept](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Intercept)* reported that a November 2023 internal memorandum by Susan Wessling and [Philip Pan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_Pan) instructed journalists to reduce using the terms "genocide" and "ethnic cleansing" and to avoid using the phrase "[occupied territory](2781576.md)" in the context of Palestinian land, "[Palestine](241405.md)" except in rare circumstances, and the term "[refugee camps](400347.md)" to describe areas of Gaza despite recognition from the United Nations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEScahillGrim2024-239] A spokesperson from the *Times* stated that issuing guidance was standard practice. An analysis by *The Intercept* noted that *The New York Times* described Israeli deaths as a massacre nearly sixty times, but had only described Palestinian deaths as a massacre once.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEScahillGrim2024-239] Writers and editors have left the newspaper due to its coverage of events in Gaza, including [Jazmine Hughes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazmine_Hughes) and [Jamie Lauren Keiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamie_Lauren_Keiles).[^cite_note-384]

In December 2023, *The New York Times* published an investigation titled "['Screams Without Words': How Hamas Weaponized Sexual Violence on Oct. 7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Screams_Without_Words)", alleging that [Hamas](13913.md) weaponized [sexual and gender-based violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_and_gender-based_violence_in_the_2023_Hamas-led_attack_on_Israel) during its [armed incursion on Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Hamas-led_attack_on_Israel).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAl_Jazeera2023-385] The investigation was the subject of an article from *The Intercept* questioning the journalistic acumen of [Anat Schwartz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anat_Schwartz), a filmmaker involved in the inquiry who had no prior reporting experience and agreed with a post stating Israel should "violate any norm, on the way to victory", doubting the veracity of the opening claim that Gal Abdush was raped in a timespan disputed by her family, and alleging that the *Times* was pressured by the [Committee for Accuracy in Middle East Reporting in America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_for_Accuracy_in_Middle_East_Reporting_in_America).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEScahillGrimBoguslaw2024-386] *The New York Times* initiated an inquiry into the leaking of confidential information about the report to other outlets, which received criticism from [NewsGuild](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NewsGuild-CWA) of New York president Susan DeCarava for purported racial targeting;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWagner2024a-387] the *Times*'s investigation was inconclusive, but found gaps in the way proprietary journalistic material is handled.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBruell2024-388]

[The New York Times Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Building) has been a site of protest action during the Gaza war and [genocide](75668427.md), including a November 2023 sit-in demanding that *The Times*'s editorial board publicly call for a ceasefire and accusing the media company of "complicity in laundering genocide",[^cite_note-389] a February 29, 2024, protest and press conference following the release of *The Intercept*'s critical investigation into the "Screams Without Words" exposé,[^cite_note-390] and an [action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_action) on July 30, 2025, in which protesters spray-painted "NYT Lies, Gaza dies" on the building's glass facade.[^cite_note-391] In addition, protesters blocked *The New York Times*'s distribution center March 14, 2024[^cite_note-392] and executive editor [Joseph Kahn's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Kahn_\(journalist\)) residence was splattered with red paint on August 25, 2025.[^cite_note-393][^cite_note-394] The collective [Writers Against the War on Gaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Writers_Against_the_War_on_Gaza), which publishes the mock publication *[The New York War Crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_War_Crimes)*, has been associated with protests against *The New York Times*.[^cite_note-395] On October 27, 2025, 300 writers—including scholars, journalists, and public intellectuals—pledged[^cite_note-396] to boycott *The New York Times* and withhold contributions to the paper in protest of what they describe as its complicity in the Gaza genocide, demanding 1) a review of anti-Palestinian bias in the newsroom, 2) a retraction of "Screams Without Words", and 3) a call from the editorial board for a US arms embargo on Israel.[^cite_note-Folta-2025-397][^cite_note-Middle_East_Eye-398][^cite_note-399][^cite_note-400] Among the initial signatories, about 150 had previously contributed to the *Times*.[^cite_note-Folta-2025-397][^cite_note-Middle_East_Eye-398]

### Transgender people

See also: [2020s anti-LGBTQ movement in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020s_anti-LGBTQ_movement_in_the_United_States) and [Persecution of transgender people under the second Trump administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_transgender_people_under_the_second_Trump_administration)

*The New York Times*' reporting on [transgender](19904525.md) issues has been criticized by the [American Academy of Pediatrics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Academy_of_Pediatrics), [World Professional Association for Transgender Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Professional_Association_for_Transgender_Health) (WPATH), [Endocrine Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endocrine_Society), and other major organizations as well as publication outlets.[^cite_note-401][^cite_note-402][^cite_note-403] When it published an opinion piece by [Weill Cornell Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weill_Cornell_Medicine) professor [Richard A. Friedman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_A._Friedman) called "How Changeable Is Gender?" in August 2015,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_York_Times2015c-404] *[Vox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vox_\(website\))*'s German Lopez criticized Friedman as suggesting that parents and doctors might be right in letting children suffer from severe dysphoria in case something changes down the line, and as implying that conversion therapy may work for transgender children.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELopez2015-405]

A January 2023 article by correspondent Katie Baker received criticism for biased reporting against transgender children, misframing the challenges school districts face when students change their gender identity without their parents' knowledge. The article failed to clarify that consequential lawsuits brought by parents against school districts are in actuality part of a legal strategy tied to groups, such as [Alliance Defending Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_Defending_Freedom), that have identified trans people as an "existential threat."[^cite_note-NPR-406][^cite_note-407][^cite_note-408][^cite_note-409]

In February 2023, nearly one thousand[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOladipo2023-410] current and former *Times* writers and contributors wrote an open letter addressed to standards editor Philip B. Corbett, criticizing the paper's coverage of transgender, [non⁠-⁠binary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-binary_gender), and [gender-nonconforming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender-nonconforming) people; some of the *Times*'s articles have been cited in state legislatures attempting to justify criminalizing gender-affirming care.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlein2023a-411] Contributors wrote in the open letter that "the *Times* has in recent years treated [gender diversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_variance) with an eerily familiar mix of [pseudoscience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pseudoscience) and euphemistic, charged language, while publishing reporting on [trans children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender_youth) that omits relevant information about its sources."[^cite_note-416] Tens of thousands of subscribers and readers also signed the letter.[^cite_note-417] One example cited was the use of the term "[patient zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Index_case)" to describe a trans child seeking transgender healthcare.[^cite_note-nbcnews.com-418] Hundreds of high-profile figures, including [Roxane Gay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roxane_Gay), [Jenna Wortham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jenna_Wortham), [Dave Itzkoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dave_Itzkoff), [Ed Yong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ed_Yong), [Chelsea Manning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chelsea_Manning), [Sarah Schulman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarah_Schulman), [Jia Tolentino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jia_Tolentino), [Lena Dunham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lena_Dunham), [Kate Zambreno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kate_Zambreno), [Gabrielle Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabrielle_Union), [Judd Apatow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judd_Apatow), [Tommy Dorfman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tommy_Dorfman), and [Cynthia Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cynthia_Nixon), signed the letter.[^cite_note-419][^cite_note-420]

That same month, over 130 LGBTQ organizations, including [GLAAD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GLAAD), the [Human Rights Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Campaign), and [PFLAG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PFLAG), released a coordinated statement which accused the *Times* of publishing coverage that ultimately spreads harmful misinformation about trans issues and promotes "fringe theories" and "dangerous inaccuracies."[^cite_note-421][^cite_note-422][^cite_note-423] As a continued effort from this statement, a digital billboard truck from GLAAD was situated outside of the *Times*’ headquarters, featuring messages such as “Dear New York Times: Stop Questioning Trans People’s Right to Exist & Access to Medical Care”.[^cite_note-WP-424][^cite_note-NPR-406][^cite_note-425]

According to former *Times* journalist Billie Jean Sweeney, a push for writers to challenge "every aspect of being trans", ranging from gender-inclusive language to access to medical care, came from the top in 2022 after leadership was handed over to [A. G. Sulzberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._G._Sulzberger), [Joe Kahn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Kahn), and [Carolyn Ryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carolyn_Ryan); as part of an effort to win good will with the Trump campaign without incurring backlash from the general populace.[^cite_note-426][^cite_note-427]

The *Times* has continually denied any bias in its reporting, stating that its coverage of "fiercely contested medical and legal debates" is fair and balanced, and that it would not tolerate journalists protesting its transgender coverage.[^cite_note-428][^cite_note-429][^cite_note-430] In June 2026, civil rights attorney [Alejandra Caraballo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejandra_Caraballo) published a study in her newsletter, *The Dissident*,[^cite_note-431] which analyzed 3,242 articles that were published in the *Times* between 2014 and 2026.[^cite_note-432] Caraballo's study concluded that from 2022 onwards, the *Times* changed the way it covered transgender issues, "moving from rights-based framing toward more skeptical, conflict-driven coverage" which also prioritized the views of opponents of transgender rights ahead of the lived experience transgender people themselves.[^cite_note-433][^cite_note-434]
